### Literature_review_Boundary_and_Initial_Conditions_2020-2026.docx  (77 reference lines)
Published in              Archive for Rational Mechanics and Analysis, 238, 47–124 (2020; issue and month not verified); arXiv 1803.02431
Published in              Numerische Mathematik, 147(1), 227–254 (January 2021; published online 2 January 2021); arXiv 2007.11828
Published in              Stochastic Processes and their Applications, 130(2), 924–961 (2020 issue; month not verified; the DOI suffix suggests the article was first available in 2019); arXiv 1806.04739
DOI / link                https://doi.org/10.1016/j.spa.2019.04.003
Published in              Advances in Computational Mathematics, 49, article 62 (published 31 July 2023; issue not verified); open access; arXiv 2107.07871
Summary. The paper proves that the 2D Boussinesq equations and the 3D axisymmetric Euler equations, posed in a domain with a solid boundary, develop a singularity in finite time from initial data whose velocity (and density, for Boussinesq) is C^(1, α). The proof uses a dynamic rescaling formulation and Elgindi's framework: an approximate self-similar profile is constructed and its linear and nonlinear stability proved; the velocity keeps finite energy up to the singular time. The blowup has the main features of the Hou–Luo numerical scenario, in which the singularity forms on the boundary where the no-flow condition holds, and the paper is the first rigorous justification of that scenario with C^(1, α) velocity. The arXiv record notes that an earlier version misstated the regularity class of the 3D velocity (the swirl component) and that this is easily fixed; a correction to the journal article appeared in 2022.

### Literature_review_Charged_Species_Transport_Hydrogeochemistry_2020-2026.docx  (55 reference lines)
BASE ARTICLE AND CONTENTS Base article: Van-Brunt, A., Farrell, P. E. & Monroe, C. W. Structural electroneutrality in Onsager–Stefan–Maxwell transport with charged species. arXiv:2211.13505 (2022); cited as Electrochimica Acta, 441, 141769 (2023). Part I: theoretical introduction with equations: the base article, the dilute Nernst–Planck limit and the diffusion potential, concentrated electrolytes and Onsager reciprocity, double layers and Donnan equilibrium, ionic diffusion in porous media, ion exchange and reactive transport, isotope fractionation by diffusion, electrokinetics and diffusiophoresis, numerical structure, and the link to coastal aquifers. Part II: 20 verified papers, 4 from each institution in the requested order: Oxford (O1–O4), Harvard (H1–H4), Caltech (C1–C4), Princeton (P1–P4) and Berkeley (BK1–BK4), each with a key equation in OMML and an innovation. Part III: synthesis and the NGARKESA-GO (ngarkesa: the charge) innovation for the SWI-Goal-OED project: which porewater and geophysical measurements best predict when a freshened coastal aquifer recovers from the salt stored in its aquitards, with electroneutral multicomponent transport as the forward model, tested numerically.
Published in   arXiv:2211.13505 (submitted 24 November 2022), https://arxiv.org/abs/2211.13505. Cited elsewhere as Electrochimica Acta, vol. 441, article 141769 (2023), DOI 10.1016/j.electacta.2022.141769; the journal details come from a secondary citation and were not verified on the publisher page.
-   Inclusion criteria: (i) peer-reviewed journal article, 1 January 2020 – 20 October 2026 (preprints, theses and conference abstracts were excluded); (ii) at least one author from the institution at the time of the paper, documented in the affiliation, an institutional repository or a group page; for Berkeley, Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory alone did not count; (iii) a direct link to at least one of the themes above; (iv) no overlap with the papers of the 28 previous literature documents of this series, checked automatically by title and DOI (no overlap was found); (v) ranking by closeness to the base article and to hydrogeochemistry, then by theoretical depth and by certainty of verification.
Affiliation         C. W. Monroe: Department of Engineering Science, University of Oxford (Monroe Research Group page). An Oxford DPhil thesis of R. Rungta (2025, ORA) lists a companion manuscript on the emergence of Nernst–Planck laws from Onsager–Stefan–Maxwell theory. The affiliation block of the article was not seen; the DOI is from an indexing site that reproduces the abstract, not from the publisher page.

### Literature_review_Complex_Nonlinear_Stochastic_Systems_Human_Body_2020-2026.docx  (54 reference lines)
DOI / link           https://doi.org/10.1016/j.media.2024.103108
DOI / link           https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cell.2022.11.027
DOI / link           https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1916367118
Summary. Many cellular responses, in gene regulation and in signaling, are switch-like, and they are routinely described with the Hill function, which was introduced more than a century ago only as an empirical fit. The paper asks what limits the sharpness of an input–output response when the molecular mechanism is at thermodynamic equilibrium; this limit is called a “Hopfield barrier”, because it can be crossed only by expending energy. The authors use the “linear framework”, that is, continuous-time Markov processes on directed graphs with labeled edges, together with a coarse-graining method, for models of arbitrary molecular complexity. The result is that, if the input ligand binds at N sites, the Hopfield barrier for sharpness is the Hill function with coefficient N, regardless of the molecular details. Data that exceed this barrier show that the mechanism expends energy to stay away from equilibrium. A 2026 paper (M. Stephan, arXiv:2606.11426) notes that part of the 2024 argument relied on numerical findings and completes it with a formal proof.

DOI / link           https://doi.org/10.1287/opre.2022.0249
Published in         Proceedings of the 2nd Conference on Learning for Dynamics and Control (L4DC), PMLR 120, 486–489 (2020); the proceedings print a short version, the full analysis is arXiv:1901.00137
Affiliation          Princeton University, Department of Operations Research and Financial Engineering (Fan), and Northwestern University, Department of Industrial Engineering and Management Sciences (Wang), as listed in the arXiv:1901.00137 rendering seen in a search result; affiliations of Xie and Yang not verified.
WHY IT IS AMONG THE BEST It put the two devices of DQN, experience replay and the target network, into a statistical theorem for deep networks, splitting the error into a geometrically vanishing algorithmic part and a nonparametric statistical part. It was cited as background in review 48 and is reviewed in full here for the first time. The published L4DC 2020 version is short; the proofs are in the arXiv version, whose algorithmic-plus-statistical template recurs in later deep value-learning analyses such as VR1.

Volume, issue, pages and month from RePEc/IDEAS (v72, i5, 1906–1927, September 2024); DOI from the INFORMS supplement link; first posted as arXiv:1906.01786 (June 2019).
WHY IT IS AMONG THE BEST It turned the observation that policy gradient finds global optima in classical control problems into a theorem, by showing that global optimality follows from two checkable structural properties shared by tabular MDPs, linear-quadratic control, optimal stopping and inventory problems, and it gave a gradient-dominance route to rates. Published in Operations Research (2024) by Columbia University authors after circulating on arXiv since 2019, it gives one explanation for several problem-specific landscape results.

### Literature_review_Information_Theory_2020-2026.docx  (38 reference lines)
DOI / link           https://doi.org/10.1103/PhysRevLett.126.190505
DOI / link           https://doi.org/10.1109/TIT.2020.2979704
Summary. Classically, when two convex sets of distributions 𝒫 and 𝒬 are tested with i.i.d. samples, the optimal error exponent is achieved by a maximum-likelihood test that depends only on the sets. The article strengthens this: even when an adversary chooses the distribution of each round after seeing the previous samples, the same exponent is achieved by a maximum-likelihood test. The model is then applied to the testing of quantum states with restricted measurements, e.g. distinguishing an entangled state from all separable states using only local operations and classical communication (LOCC). The entanglement between copies is simulated by an adaptive classical adversary, and the result is a quantum Stein's lemma in which the optimal rate is a relative entropy restricted to the class of measurements. As a by-product, a new proof is given of the Li–Winter strengthening of strong subadditivity. The journal version was published in 2020, but the work started earlier (arXiv 2013).

Note on verification: the bibliographic data were checked against public online sources (publisher websites, DBLP, PMLR, NeurIPS, JMLR, Project Euclid, institutional repositories, PubMed Central, arXiv). For conference articles and JMLR articles, the publication link is given instead of a DOI. The article by Korda & Mezić (2020) in IEEE TAC was left out because its DOI could not be confirmed. For some articles, the affiliations were taken from the arXiv versions or from institutional repositories and may differ slightly from the published version. The equations marked “representative formulation” summarize the result in unified notation and are not verbatim copies.
### Literature_review_M-estimators_Adaptive_Data_2020-2026.docx  (123 reference lines)
| Every paper was re-read from scratch on October 18–19, 2026. The pages of arXiv, of the NeurIPS and PMLR proceedings, of Project Euclid, PNAS, OUP and PMC were blocked in the working environment. We therefore used the authors' versions on GitHub (manuscripts, LaTeX source, simulation code), the official PMLR repository on GitHub and web search. A bibliographic field is marked as verified only when a source states it explicitly; otherwise it is marked "(not verified)". Changes from the previous version of the document are given under each paper. |
Bibliographic record. Kelly W. Zhang, Lucas Janson and Susan A. Murphy, Statistical Inference with M-Estimators on Adaptively Collected Data. Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 34 (NeurIPS 2021), pp. 7460–7471, December 2021. arXiv 2104.14074: v1 on April 29, 2021, titled Statistical Inference with M-Estimators on Bandit Data; v2 on May 28, 2021; v3, the latest version, on November 20, 2021, with the published title. The authors' manuscript is in PubMed Central: PMID 35757490, PMCID PMC9232184, NIHMS1762664. OpenReview: TJOQw_vMlAj. DOI: no source gives a DOI (not verified). We found no journal version or erratum, but this was not verified either way. The public code is at github.com/kellywzhang/adaptively_weighted_Mestimation (GPL-3.0 license, three commits, all on August 30, 2022); we did not verify whether the paper itself refers to this repository. Equation numbering may differ between the arXiv v3, NeurIPS and PMC versions: according to an excerpt, a self-normalized display is numbered (4) in the NeurIPS PDF. Below, therefore, the paper's equations are cited by content, not by number.
Bibliographic record. Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 33 (NeurIPS 2020), pages 9818–9829, 2020. arXiv:2002.03217 [cs.LG]: v1 on February 8, 2020, v2 on May 18, 2020, v3 on January 8, 2021 (the latest version, with the note "NeurIPS 2020"). Author manuscript in PMC: PMC8734616; PMID 35002190. DOI: no checked source gives a DOI (not verified). The NeurIPS reviews are public. Reproduction code, written on February 8–11, 2020 (the v1 period): https://github.com/kellywzhang/inference_batched_bandits.
Bibliographic record. Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 34 (NeurIPS 2021), pages 28548–28559, December 2021; the pages and the month come from the authors' manuscript in PubMed Central (PMC9249103), not from the proceedings page. arXiv:2106.00418, v1 on June 1, 2021; later versions were not verified. Other records: NSF PAR 10320790, OpenReview 405l3VpbqRA, NeurIPS poster 27658 and the supplement with the proofs on the proceedings page. No publisher DOI was found; the ACM Digital Library identifier 10.5555/3540261.3542448 is internal and is not cited as a DOI. No code repository was found.
The target is the policy value, a scalar functional, not the parameter of an M-estimator. In the language of this document, CADR is the Z-estimator with score σ̂_(t)⁻¹(D_(t)−Ψ), and Γ̂_(T)⁻¹ plays the role of ${\ddot{M}}_{T}({\widehat{\theta}}_{T})$ (up to sign, since the derivative of the mean score with respect to Ψ is  − Γ̂_(T)⁻¹): random, but observable and inside the statistic. A clear gain concerns misspecification: thanks to the known policy, the score is unbiased for every outcome model, whereas the conditionally defined target of the base paper requires a correct model. The loss, in our reading, is that only a mean functional is treated, with more technical conditions. The two papers appeared almost simultaneously (the base paper on arXiv in April 2021, [2] on June 1, 2021), and it was not verified whether [2] cites the base paper. According to the authors, A5–A6 are "slightly more stringent" than the conditions of a prior approach that "requires asymptotic stabilization of the logging policies". This approach was not identified; we note that it is not the base paper, whose weights do not require π_(t) to converge.
-   Follow-up work. The preprint Statistical Inference for Continuous Action Bandits (arXiv:2610.09801) cites [2]: its exploration assumption requires a lower bound polynomial in t, as in another 2021 paper and in [2], and paraphrases it as π_(t, a) ≥ t^(−1/2). In our reconstruction, t^(−1/2) is exactly the boundary that A3 excludes. The preprints Valid Post-Contextual Bandit Inference (arXiv:2505.13897) and Statistical Inference for Misspecified Contextual Bandits (arXiv:2509.06287) came up in the searches for [2]; it was not verified whether they cite it.
Bibliographic record. Journal of the European Economic Association 22(2), pp. 781–836, 2024. The volume, issue and first page come from the URL of the publisher's page (academic.oup.com/jeea/article-abstract/22/2/781/7424979), which was not opened. The last page, the article number jvad067, the DOI 10.1093/jeea/jvad067 and the online publication date, November 16, 2023, come only from the summary of a search engine that drew on institutional repositories (not verified). Pre-registration: AEA RCT Registry, https://doi.org/10.1257/rct.3870-2.2. Working-paper versions, confirmed only at the URL level: CEPR DP15359, Warwick TWERP 1335 (2021), CAGE DP 547, CESifo WP 8535 and a J-PAL version (October 2020). No arXiv version was found, but its absence was not verified. The source read is the accepted manuscript in the journal class (May 4, 2023, 62 pages; [MS] below) with the Online Appendix (April 14, 2023, 36 pages; [OA]). The print version was not opened, so sections, pages and equation numbers refer to [MS]. Assignment code: github.com/maxkasy/ThompsonHierarchicalApp.
Bibliographic record. Econometrica, vol. 89, no. 1 (January 2021), pp. 113–132. DOI: 10.3982/ECTA17527. The manuscript was received on July 27, 2019, accepted on August 23, 2020 and published online on September 24, 2020; the co-editor was Guido Imbens. The Supplement has the same DOI (the authors' copy is dated August 24, 2020). No arXiv version was found; the working-paper version CESifo WP 7778 (2019) is not verified. Kasy's website hosts a four-page correction note by the authors, dated November 10, 2021, Correction regarding 'Adaptive treatment assignment in experiments for policy choice', linked there as "Correction regarding Theorem 1, Item 3". We found no erratum in Econometrica itself, and the formal publication of the comment by Ariu, Kato, Komiyama & McAlinn (2021) could not be confirmed.
The original version and the error. In the article, items 1–2 had convergence in probability, item 2 claimed $- \frac{1}{NT}\text{log}\text{ }p_{T}^{d} \rightarrow \Gamma^{\star}$ for every d ≠ d⁽¹⁾, and item 3 claimed $- \frac{1}{NT}\text{log}\text{ }R_{\theta}(T) \rightarrow \Gamma^{\star}$, as well as that no algorithm with ρ̂ ≠ ρ and ρ̂^(d⁽¹⁾) = 1/2 drives R_(θ)(T) to zero faster. The comment by Ariu et al. (2021), posted on arXiv on September 16, 2021, shows, building on Carpentier & Locatelli (2016), that a counterexample to item 3 exists: frequentist regret goes to zero more slowly than claimed. According to the authors' note, the existence proof requires more than exp(800) ≈ 10³⁴⁷ arms. According to snippets of the abstract in search results (not verified), the first version of the comment calls the proofs of items 1–2 incorrect but adds that the statements themselves may hold, while the later version also calls the statement of item 2 incorrect. The authors' note traces the source to Lemma 2(1), $\text{lim}\text{ } - \frac{1}{NT}\text{logℙ}({\widehat{\theta}}_{T}^{d} > {\widehat{\theta}}_{T}^{d^{(1)}}) = \Gamma^{d}$, and gives two reasons:
Bibliographic record. Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 38 (NeurIPS 2025), Main Conference Track, poster; year 2025. Volume 38 was confirmed only by a search summary. arXiv:2511.06639 [math.ST], v1 of November 10, 2025 (only v1 was found). Pages 112252–112281 (not verified) and DOI 10.52202/085713-3750 (not verified) come from the previous version; the DOI matches the Curran Associates file "085713-3750open.pdf", but the DOI string itself was not seen on any page, so both fields are omitted from the reference. No public code was found.
Authors and affiliations. Kevin Du and Lucas Janson, Department of Statistics, Harvard University; Yash Nair, Department of Statistics, Stanford University (according to the header of the arXiv PDF). The precursor of the paper is Du's (2025) A.B. thesis at Harvard, advised by Janson. According to its abstract, the posterior under adaptive sampling is approximately normal, centered at the maximum likelihood estimator (MLE) and with covariance equal to the inverse Fisher information, while the resulting Bayesian uncertainty quantification may not be asymptotically frequentist-valid in some adaptive settings. At the time of publication Janson was at Harvard; according to his CV, as of September 1, 2026 he moves to the Department of Statistics at Stanford.
Bibliographic record. The Annals of Statistics, volume 49, issue 2, pages 1055–1080, April 2021. DOI: 10.1214/20-AOS1991. arXiv:1810.08240, first posted in 2018; version v9 exists, but whether it is the latest was not verified. Early arXiv versions and a McGill seminar on February 22, 2019 carried the title Uniform, nonparametric, non-asymptotic confidence sequences. Project Euclid lists the paper as open access, and a copy is available in the NSF PAR repository. The first author maintains the confseq library (C++ with Python and R interfaces). Its README cites the Annals version and uses its numbering: Theorem 1 for stitching, Corollary 2 for the average treatment effect, Propositions 4–9 for mixtures and Section 3.5 for tuning. Equation numbers are not used, because the Annals PDF and arXiv v9 were not compared.

| Here D is the Bregman divergence of the regularized log-partition function, Γ_(t)^(ν) is the Bregman information gain defined by Chowdhury et al. (2022), which for a Gaussian likelihood with unit weights coincides with the classical information gain, and ν is the regularization parameter (our interpretation). The ingredients are confirmed by a summary of the arXiv PDF; the placement of the bracket is not. The previous version's reading, $\frac{4L}{\mu}(\xi_{t} + 2\text{log}(1/\delta))$, gives c_(δ) = 8L/μ; the other reading gives c_(δ) = 2. In both readings the dominant dependence is (L/μ)(log(1/α)+Γ_(t)^(ν)) + ℛ_(t). The regret bounds for FTRL were not verified. |
Limitations and open problems. (i) Correct specification is a condition for validity, not only for efficiency; the abstract calls the method "especially suitable for problems with well-specified likelihoods". (ii) The size depends on the sequence of estimators, and the nonasymptotic size analysis covers only GLMs; for RKHS classes coverage holds, but there is no size bound. (iii) Lee, Yun & Jun (NeurIPS 2024) note that the radii of generic confidence sequences for convex GLMs, including [7], carry a worst-case curvature factor; for the logistic model it grows roughly like e^(B) (see the values of L/μ above). (iv) Non-identifiability (our remark, not verified in the paper): C_(t) contains every θ with the same likelihood. When the hydraulic heads depend only on the ratio of recharge to transmissivity, C_(t) stretches along the whole curve of constant ratio inside Θ, so the interval for each parameter separately stays as wide as Θ allows, while the projection onto an identifiable functional may be narrow. (v) Projecting a d-dimensional set onto a single functional is conservative, because it covers all functionals simultaneously. (vi) The bracket in Theorem 3 was not resolved. A search summary without a confirmed source mentioned a problem in Theorem 3, later resolved without changing the set; this was not confirmed and, in any case, does not affect Theorem 1. Before the constants of Theorem 3 are used, the latest version of arXiv:2311.04402 and the appendix of arXiv:2407.13977 must be checked. Among follow-up papers, Lee, Yun & Jun (AISTATS 2024) note that [7] uses FTRL estimators with bias-based reweighting; Clerico et al. (2025) cite it and generalize the step from regret to confidence set; Kirschner, Krause, Meziu & Mutný (2025), from the same group, replace the plug-in estimator with likelihood mixing. No erratum was found.

It differs from the text in the prefactor, $1/\sqrt{\kappa\lambda}$ instead of $\sqrt{2\kappa/\lambda}$, and it replaces γ_(t) with a realized, unnormalized log-determinant, logdet(K_(t)+κλI) rather than logdet(I+K_(t)/(κλ)). This does not show what Theorem 10 says and must not be used to "correct" the paper. (vi) On the 100-arm domains the code solves the max–min problem exactly; approximation matters only on continuous domains. No follow-up paper citing [8] was verified, and no erratum was found. Related works, not confirmed to cite it, include A Unified Confidence Sequence for Generalized Linear Models, with Applications to Bandits (arXiv:2407.13977, NeurIPS 2024) and arXiv:2604.22161 (2026); according to a search extract, the latter notes that kernelized or neural logistic extensions do not admit an analogous bound. Open problems, in our assessment: kernelized bands with a weaker dependence on κ, an unknown B, and a target defined under misspecification.

Bibliographic record. International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR 2021), spotlight presentation (there is also a poster page). ICLR proceedings have no volume, issue or pages; no source gives a DOI, and the OpenReview identifier was not found. arXiv:2101.11665 (stat): v1 on January 27, 2021, v2 on May 31, 2021; v2 is the latest version seen, but a later version was not ruled out. -   The CELEUS preprint (arXiv:2606.20820), which was not read, uses coefficients with (N−m)(N−m+1) in the denominator inside an e-process, which connects [11] to route B. No correction of [11] was found, but a dedicated search was not carried out.
Novelty status. PURE and LURE are from [11]; the unbiased variance estimator for the coefficients c_(m), the design rule that preserves the unifying condition, and the embedding in the switchback tests of KAUZ-OED are our proposal. Unlike PESHA-GO, which weights the estimating equation of the M-estimator to handle adaptive assignment (square-root weights W_(t) for a model believed correct, full weights W_(t)² under misspecification), LEVEL-LAB handles a second layer of randomness, the within-period laboratory subsampling, with full weights and leveling, and shows how it enters the sandwich variance of PESHA-GO. We did not find it in the literature we read, but CELEUS (arXiv:2606.20820), which we did not read, uses coefficients of the c_(m) type inside an e-process; the search was not systematic, so verify before presenting it as novel.

Here $N_{i,t,a,\eta} = \sum_{j}^{}\mathbf{1}(\rho_{i,j}^{(a)} \leq \eta,\ A_{j,t} = a)$ is the number of neighbors. No propensity enters the estimator (the 2022 materials and the nsquared package, arXiv:2506.04166, which implements it). Still open for the published version: whether time t is excluded from the distance (the March 2022 poster and the code exclude it, while the October 2022 poster sums over t′ = 1, …, T), whether i can be its own neighbor, and the no-neighbor case. For this case the code returns the observed outcome when (i,t) is observed, and otherwise the mean of all outcomes observed under a at time t. The threshold is compared with the noise level. -   Completeness. The authors defend it by citing Wang, Foster & Kakade (2020): realizability alone may require an exponential number of samples. Without completeness, the results still bound the statistical error of θ̂, while the approximation error is handled with the classical results on approximate value iteration; neither the variance formula nor convergence to v_(π) is then guaranteed (our reading). Two later preprints remove completeness (arXiv:2512.23805, arXiv:2607.05375), and arXiv:2406.10438 (ICML 2024) analyzes FQE beyond parametric models; whether they cite [16] was not verified.

### Literature_review_OED_Transport_Maps_Extensions_Contacts_2020-2026.docx  (61 reference lines)
Published in      Inverse Problems, vol. 40, no. 12, published 17 October 2024 (article number not verified); arXiv 2401.07971 (v1 15 January 2024, v3 17 August 2024)
Whom to contact   Karina Koval (first author; Heidelberg project page: https://scoop.iwr.uni-heidelberg.de/team/kkoval) and Robert Scheichl (Heidelberg; page not verified). Koval has since presented a scalable sequential version that combines transport maps, a derivative-based bound on the information and likelihood-informed subspaces, with groundwater-flow examples (Cambridge talk listing; the related preprint with Cui, Herzog and Scheichl is arXiv 2502.20086, not peer-reviewed).
-   Limits of verification: publisher pages, Crossref and arXiv could not be opened directly in this environment; all data were verified from search-engine summaries of these pages, and no full text was read. The key equations are therefore representative formulations, and the classical references of Part I are marked as not verified. Positions and pages of the contacts were checked by search in October 2026; positions change, and the cards say where a title or page could not be confirmed.
-   Inclusion criteria: (i) peer-reviewed journal article or paper in the proceedings of NeurIPS, ICML, ICLR or AISTATS, 1 January 2020 – 20 October 2026 (preprints were excluded); (ii) for the five institutions, at least one author there at the time of the paper; (iii) a direct link to the themes above; (iv) no overlap with the papers of the 30 previous literature documents of this series, checked automatically by title and DOI; many central papers of the field (for example on monotone triangular maps, deep adaptive design, ensemble Kalman methods and density-based estimators of the information) were excluded for this reason; (v) ranking by closeness to the base article, then by theoretical depth and certainty of verification.
-   Where the gaps are: no peer-reviewed 2020–2026 paper from Harvard on Bayesian experimental design itself was found that is not already in the series, so both Harvard papers are theory papers that carry over to design (parallel submodular selection and unbiased MCMC). The Princeton papers come from one group (Mengdi Wang) and concern generative transport rather than design criteria. A goal-oriented design paper by Zhong, Shen, Catanach and Huan (arXiv 2403.18072) is very close to the topic, but its journal version could not be confirmed and it appears only in further reading.
